# Experiment Notes

2026-10-22 The 0.005 weight-decay encoder completed training and clean validation with training macro F1 0.738277, clean-validation accuracy 0.960556, and clean-validation macro F1 0.720567. Its aggregate black-box test measurement was missing, so it was queued before any new experiment. Selection remains clean-validation-only.

## 2026-10-22: Refill queue after verified exhaustion handoff

The watchdog completed the end-to-end exhaustion transition. It recorded queue exhaustion, set `awaiting_codex_planning`, and sent a planning request to the live Codex thread. Codex reviewed the completed Qwen 1.5B two-epoch official test result of 0.651424 accuracy and 0.460927 macro F1, then replaced the empty queue with two clean-validation encoder probes. Each probe has its aggregate-only official test evaluation immediately after training.

## 2026-10-22: Require queue visibility after guidance and queue changes

Updated `auto-research-prompt.md` to require the agent to print the complete active and pending worker queue after every user guidance message and after every queue mutation. The prompt now requires explicit empty-queue and awaiting-planning status so the user can verify the exact execution state.

## 2026-10-20: Clarify mandatory non-idle execution

Updated `auto-research-prompt.md` to state in explicit terms that the research agent must not remain idle after an experiment, interruption, status request, failure, or result. The agent must immediately analyze, record, and launch the next useful experiment. The loop may wait only for an active process or required external operation and may stop only on an exact top-level `STOP` message.

The watchdog audit found a gap: it logged an active state with no worker but did not start the declared command. Updated the recovery condition so an active state with no worker starts the worker command. The fixed split replication exited without an artifact and is queued for automatic recovery.

Expanded the prompt with an explicit watchdog recovery rule. When active research has no worker, stale heartbeat, crash, or unexpected termination, the watchdog must load durable state, identify the next unfinished experiment, start or restart the worker, write the PID and heartbeat, and record recovery. It must never only log and wait for a user message.

## 2026-10-20: Establish clean When2Call research protocol

Saved the autonomous research prompt in `auto-research-prompt.md`. Audited the repository and found that historical When2Call routing experiments loaded official test labels and used test metrics to select routing rules, ensembles, and checkpoints. Marked those results as historical and excluded them from new model selection. Added a deterministic stratified development split from `train_pref`, a one epoch Jina baseline runner that never loads the official test split, durable research state, a validation leaderboard, a research summary, and a five minute watchdog with state based recovery. The first experiment is the clean baseline audit.

## 2026-09-11: Add cached LLM output verifier experiment

Added experiments/llm_output_verifier.py to select one of the existing ten EvalPlus candidates per task with one external LLM call. The script includes the task prompt, all standard and extended test inputs, candidate programs, and saved observed outputs while withholding correctness labels. It caches each decision by prompt hash and computes accuracy only after selection so the artifact can be reused for follow-up verifier experiments.

The initial API attempt was rejected because gpt-5-mini does not support an explicit temperature of zero. Removed that unsupported parameter before rerunning.

The first corrected run issued verifier requests sequentially and was interrupted after two completed cached calls. Changed the runner to use a configurable thread pool with five concurrent gpt-5-mini requests while preserving incremental per-task caching.

The parallel run completed 20 official EvalPlus tasks with five concurrent gpt-5-mini API calls. The verifier selected one saved candidate per task and reached 11/20 base accuracy, 10/20 MBPP+ accuracy, and 10/20 combined accuracy. The matched ordinary candidate-prefix curves were computed after selection for comparison.

Started a two-task preview for a different method that asks gpt-5-mini to predict only the returned values for the three standard MBPP tests. The preview compares those predictions with saved candidate outputs and does not run the full experiment.

The first preview comparison falsely reported no matching candidates because native JSON predictions were compared directly with the saved typed output encoding. Corrected the conversion and recomputed matches from the cached responses without additional API calls.

Corrected a reporting mistake in the preview interpretation. The GPT-5 mini predictions for Mbpp/782 were mathematically correct as [14, 15, 8]. The saved candidate outputs were [2, 4, 7], so no candidate matched.

Invalidated the two-task standard-answer preview as an independent experiment. The predictor received the raw MBPP prompt containing the visible assertion assert odd_length_sum([1,2,4]) == 14. The copied EvalPlus Qwen generator also passes this raw prompt into its ChatML user message, so the original candidate generations saw the same example answer. A valid rerun must remove visible assertion lines from both sides before prediction and matching.

The approved full prediction run will use the same raw MBPP prompt condition, start with 32 concurrent gpt-5-mini requests, halve concurrency after any API rate limit, cache each successful response, and compute accuracy against trusted canonical outputs on all stored standard base tests. The current artifact has 369 tasks with three base tests and 30 tasks with four to seven base tests. All 399 API responses completed at 32 workers without rate limiting. Reporting first encountered an unsupported canonical regex Match return type and is now tolerant of such outputs.

Completed all 399 gpt-5-mini standard-output predictions at 32 concurrent workers. GPT-5 mini got 1,091 of 1,236 stored base outputs correct, for 88.27 percent per-test accuracy, and got every stored base output correct on 342 of 399 tasks, for 85.71 percent task accuracy. Saved raw responses, prompts, predictions, ground truth, and candidate matching indices in the full prediction artifact.

Added an answer-independent reranker that scores each of the ten saved candidates by the fraction of stored base-test outputs matching GPT-5 mini predictions. It ranks by score and preserves original candidate order for ties, then computes Pass@K after ranking.

Completed GPT prediction reranking on all 399 tasks. Reranked Pass@1 improved from 51.38 to 63.91 percent on base, from 43.61 to 51.63 percent on MBPP+, and from 42.86 to 50.63 percent on combined correctness. Pass@10 was unchanged at 66.42 percent base and 55.89 percent combined.

Added local Qwen answer-prediction evaluation for the 1.5B, 3B, and 7B Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct checkpoints. It uses the same system and user messages as the GPT-5 mini predictor, predicts test outputs only, and measures against canonical outputs.

Added a cohort analysis for GPT-5 mini answer prediction. It groups tasks by the number of correct saved candidates among ten, then reports prediction accuracy for mixed, all-correct, and all-incorrect candidate sets.

Corrected the cohort analysis target after clarification. The requested cohort breakdown is now recorded for the completed Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct answer-prediction run: 55.06 percent per-test accuracy on mixed tasks, 71.10 percent on all-correct tasks, and 47.23 percent on all-incorrect tasks.

## September 8, 2026: Balance verifier training classes

The verifier trainer now reports task and class distributions, logs those distributions to W&B at step zero, and optionally downsamples only the overrepresented training class to an exact 50/50 split. The rerun uses 7,890 saved generated candidates from 789 synthetic tasks, while validation remains the unchanged official EvalPlus generated-candidate artifact with 3,990 candidates from 399 tasks.

## Session

- Date: August 13, 2026.
- Goal: Improve held-out MBPP pass@1 for Qwen3.5-0.8B using 299 training problems and 75 held-out problems.

## Starting Point

The base model scored 22/75, or 29.3 percent pass@1. The project used MBPP as a small and inexpensive environment for iterating on post-training methods.

## Cycle 1: Make GRPO produce a usable learning signal

### Hypothesis

A binary full-pass reward was too sparse for the weak base model because groups in which every candidate failed gave GRPO no relative preference signal.

### Experiment

The reward was decomposed into format, syntax, interface, execution, and partial test-progress components. Group-level reward variance and component metrics were added to W&B and local logs.

### Metrics

- The first dense-reward run produced nonzero reward variance and gradients on all five steps.
- Four steps had mixed rewards in every group. The remaining step had mixed rewards in seven of eight groups.
- Held-out pass@1 moved from 21/75 to 19/75 in that short run.

### What Went Well

The dense reward eliminated the all-zero learning signal and made partial progress observable.

### What Went Badly

Five optimizer steps were not enough to improve held-out correctness, and a usable gradient did not guarantee a directionally useful update.

### Learning

Reward variance is necessary for GRPO but is not sufficient for generalization. The direction of the shaped reward must also align with full correctness.

### Next Hypothesis

Give dense-reward GRPO a longer training budget and retain the best intermediate checkpoint because performance may not improve monotonically.

## Cycle 2: Train dense-reward GRPO longer

### Hypothesis

The dense reward would improve MBPP if the policy received enough updates, and checkpoint selection would preserve gains lost by later updates.

### Experiment

GRPO trained for ten epochs with four generations per prompt, an effective batch size of 32, and checkpoint evaluation every 25 optimizer steps.

### Metrics

- An earlier GRPO result scored 22/75, or 29.3 percent pass@1.
- Checkpoint 150 scored 30/75, or 40 percent pass@1.
- The final training checkpoint scored 26/75, or 34.7 percent pass@1.

### What Went Well

The longer budget produced an 11 percentage point absolute improvement over 29.3 percent, and best-checkpoint restoration preserved it.

### What Went Badly

Checkpoint performance was nonmonotonic. Using the final weights would have discarded much of the gain.

### Learning

Held-out evaluation and checkpoint selection are core parts of the training method rather than optional reporting.

### Next Hypothesis

Warm-start GRPO with supervised fine-tuning so the model begins RL with basic coding competence.

## Cycle 3: Add response-only SFT before GRPO

### Hypothesis

SFT should quickly teach function signatures, valid Python, output format, and common solution patterns before GRPO explores execution outcomes.

### Experiment

An optional response-only SFT stage was added before GRPO. Prompt tokens were masked from the cross-entropy loss. W&B logged per-batch loss, cumulative average loss, and moving-average loss.

### Metrics

- A five-batch smoke run completed successfully.
- Its batch losses were 1.374, 1.006, 1.036, 1.371, and 0.9086.
- The final cumulative average loss was 1.139.

### What Went Well

The SFT implementation trained on verified MBPP reference solutions and passed the focused test suite.

### What Went Badly

The first SFT-only run failed before training because baseline evaluation expected `results_log_path`, which was missing from the debug configuration.

### Learning

An SFT-only execution path should skip unrelated baseline work, and stage-specific smoke tests need complete logging configuration.

### Next Hypothesis

Evaluate the model before SFT and after each full epoch to measure whether the warm start improves held-out pass@1.

## Cycle 4: Evaluate one full SFT epoch

### Hypothesis

One supervised pass through the 299 training examples would produce a large early competence gain.

### Experiment

The base model was evaluated on all 75 held-out problems, trained for one SFT epoch with approximately ten optimizer batches, and evaluated again. The final SFT evaluation was designed to become the GRPO starting measurement without duplicate evaluation.

### Metrics

- Initial pass@1 was 22/75, or 29.3 percent.
- Post-SFT pass@1 was 27/75, or 36 percent.
- Format errors fell from four to one.
- Final batch loss was 0.6242, and cumulative epoch loss was 0.8150.

### What Went Well

One epoch produced a 6.7 percentage point absolute gain, or approximately a 23 percent relative improvement.

### What Went Badly

The 75-example evaluation is coarse because one problem changes pass@1 by approximately 1.33 percentage points.

### Learning

SFT efficiently captured an easy initial gain from direct demonstrations.

### Next Hypothesis

Continue SFT until held-out performance saturates, then apply GRPO to the strongest supervised checkpoint.

## Cycle 5: Extend SFT toward saturation

### Hypothesis

More supervised epochs might continue the rapid first-epoch improvement before reaching a practical ceiling.

### Experiment

SFT was configured for 100 epochs with a held-out evaluation after every epoch.

### Metrics

- The run was manually stopped during the epoch 24 evaluation.
- Training loss continued to decrease.
- Held-out pass@1 declined after the initial improvement.

### What Went Well

Per-epoch evaluation made the onset of overfitting visible.

### What Went Badly

The 299-example training set was small enough for supervised optimization to specialize quickly. Continuing toward 100 epochs consumed compute while evaluation performance deteriorated.

### Learning

One SFT epoch was useful, but extended SFT overfit this dataset. A lower SFT training loss was not evidence of better generalization.

### Next Hypothesis

Use exactly one SFT epoch as a warm start and let GRPO optimize execution outcomes afterward.

## Cycle 6: Run one SFT epoch followed by GRPO

### Hypothesis

SFT would provide the easy competence gain, and GRPO would then improve beyond imitation by rewarding any correct program.

### Experiment

The model trained for one SFT epoch and then entered the existing dense-reward GRPO stage with four generations per prompt. The post-SFT evaluation was reused at the GRPO boundary.

### Metrics

- Post-SFT pass@1 was 27/75, or 36 percent.
- GRPO reached 31/75, or 41.3 percent, at checkpoints 175, 350, and 425.
- GRPO without SFT had previously reached 30/75, or 40 percent.
- The active SFT plus GRPO run later fluctuated below its peak. Checkpoint 775 scored 29/75, or 38.7 percent.

### What Went Well

GRPO improved the SFT model from 36 percent to a 41.3 percent best checkpoint.

### What Went Badly

The improvement over no-SFT GRPO was only one held-out problem. Continued training did not preserve the peak.

### Learning

SFT changed how quickly the model reached useful performance but did not clearly raise the final ceiling. The 40 percent and 41.3 percent results should be treated as effectively tied without repeated seeds.

### Next Hypothesis

Understand why cumulative training reward continued increasing while held-out pass@1 stagnated.

## Cycle 7: Diagnose reward and evaluation divergence

### Hypothesis

GRPO was increasingly optimizing partial progress on the 299 training problems rather than full correctness on unseen problems.

### Experiment

The latest W&B run, `autumn-wave-23` with run ID `qwv7wv0d`, was inspected for cumulative reward components, held-out evaluations, group variance, and full-pass behavior.

### Metrics

- `training/average_reward` rose from approximately 0.253 near step 21 to approximately 0.336 near step 645.
- `training/average_reward/test_progress` rose from approximately 0.090 to 0.139 and contributed the largest increase.
- Held-out pass@1 peaked at 41.3 percent and later remained mostly between 36 and 40 percent.
- At sampled trainer step 720, `reward/mean_group_std` was approximately 0.190.
- At that step, `reward/mixed_group_fraction` was 1.0 and `reward/flat_group_fraction` was 0.
- Across 30 sampled W&B points, mixed-group fraction was usually 0.875 or 1.0, while flat-group fraction was usually 0 or 0.125.

### What Went Well

Four generations usually produced healthy within-prompt reward differences, so GRPO retained comparative signal.

### What Went Badly

The cumulative reward average hid recent deterioration, partial test progress could rise without full passage, and training repeatedly reused the same 299 prompts and tests. GRPO also trained on sampled candidates while evaluation used one greedy completion.

### Learning

Insufficient exploration was not the clearest bottleneck. The evidence pointed more strongly to training-set specialization and reward misalignment with held-out full correctness.

### Next Hypothesis

Keep four generations and test a dynamic reward that begins with dense partial credit but gradually shifts most weight toward full passage.

## Cycle 8: Automate plateau stopping

### Hypothesis

Automatically stopping after five checkpoints without a new pass@1 maximum would prevent wasted compute and preserve the best checkpoint.

### Experiment

A plateau watcher and RunPod stop supervisor were prepared for the active training process. The supervisor verified the pod through the RunPod REST API before it could become armed.

### Metrics

- The active run exceeded five consecutive checkpoints without a new maximum.
- RunPod pod verification returned HTTP 403 twice.

### What Went Well

The supervisor failed closed and did not interrupt training without verified pod-control authorization.

### What Went Badly

The injected `RUNPOD_API_KEY` existed but could not control the current pod. The automatic stop schedule had to be disarmed.

### Learning

Durable stopping automation depends on verifying external control permissions before relying on it. Local plateau detection and remote pod control should remain separate gates.

### Next Hypothesis

Fix RunPod authorization or use a reliable local process-stop mechanism, then run the dynamic-reward experiment with early stopping and best-checkpoint restoration.

## Agentic Coding

### Skills Created or Updated

- The `take-meeting-notes` skill was created to turn long working sessions into durable notes. Commit `db40517` added the skill.
- The skill was revised to organize notes as chronological hypothesis and experiment cycles. Commit `0e67c96` added the cycle format.
- The skill was revised again to require a dedicated Agentic Coding section for skills, automation, orchestration, repository changes, failures, and reusable lessons.

### Workflows and Automation

- The `run-iteratively` skill governed the five-batch SFT smoke test and the full-epoch SFT validation. Each loop used an explicit stopping condition and preserved failed and successful run evidence.
- The `runpod-auto-stop` skill separated local plateau detection from remote pod shutdown. The supervisor remained disarmed when RunPod authorization could not be verified.
- The `publish-blog-post` workflow kept the tiny LLM article synchronized with measured SFT and GRPO findings. Website commits included `007f921`, `6f8d45e`, `699d12a`, and `8985514`.
- Parallel agents inspected SFT interfaces, designed checkpoint evaluation, analyzed performance options, and updated the blog. The main agent integrated and verified their outputs.
- The W&B connector was used to inspect the active run directly instead of inferring behavior from summary logs alone.

### Code and Infrastructure Changes

- Commit `ea5020d` added response-only SFT, stage configuration, and W&B loss metrics for the current batch, cumulative average, and moving average.
- Commits `51e5604`, `75eb586`, and `e438152` fixed the SFT-only execution path and recorded failed and successful smoke runs.
- Commit `1d29cd5` added initial and per-epoch SFT evaluation while preventing duplicate evaluation at the SFT to GRPO boundary. Commit `4e31d8a` recorded the successful full-epoch run.
- Commits `2ab7715` and `0bda995` captured the extended SFT experiment, stopped the overfitting run, and restored the one-epoch warm start.
- Commits `115a078` and `dfec620` created and then restructured this durable notes file.

### What Worked

- Explicit stopping conditions made training validation concrete. Five successful SFT batches and one completed epoch each had an unambiguous completion test.
- Stage-specific evaluation and metric logging exposed overfitting early and prevented duplicated boundary evaluations.
- Parallel read-only investigation reduced implementation latency while isolated edit ownership avoided file conflicts.
- Small commits linked code, run artifacts, and conclusions into a reproducible history.

### What Failed

- The first SFT-only smoke run failed because the debug configuration omitted a logging path required by baseline evaluation.
- RunPod automatic shutdown could not be armed because pod verification returned HTTP 403.
- A skills repository push was rejected because the remote branch had advanced. Rebasing resolved the divergence.
- One repository push encountered transient GitHub DNS failure. A retry succeeded.

### Learning

Agentic workflows are most reliable when each loop has a measurable stopping condition, each external action fails closed, and every conclusion points to a durable metric, artifact, or commit. Skills should capture reusable process, while session notes should preserve how that process actually behaved.

## Session

- Date: August 14, 2026.
- Topic: Expand MBPP training coverage with hidden synthetic tests.

## Starting Point

The official MBPP training split contained 374 tasks. Reusing the visible assertions risked specializing the reward to narrow inputs, so the next experiment added programmatically generated tests without adding new task families.

## Cycle 9: Generate hidden tests from existing tasks

### Hypothesis

Mutating existing test inputs and evaluating them with trusted reference solutions would broaden reward coverage without the cost or semantic drift of LLM-generated tasks.

### Experiment

A deterministic generator created up to 20 additional tests per training task. The training loader optionally appended these tests to execution-only test code while keeping them out of model prompts and leaving validation unchanged.

### Metrics

- The generator produced 6,762 hidden tests across 374 tasks.
- A total of 260 tasks reached the requested 20 tests.
- Nine tasks produced no safe variants.
- All 30 repository tests passed.

### What Went Well

The generated tests expanded input coverage while preserving the existing task-family distribution and official validation split.

### What Went Badly

Some tasks did not support 20 distinct safe mutations, and nine tasks produced no accepted variants.

### Learning

Reference-backed mutation can cheaply widen tests, but safe programmatic diversity is limited by the structure and domains of the original assertions.

### Next Hypothesis

Confirm that GRPO loads and executes the hidden tests during an actual optimizer step.

## Cycle 10: Validate synthetic tests in one GRPO step

### Hypothesis

An explicit training metric would demonstrate that hidden tests reached reward execution rather than only loading successfully.

### Experiment

A one-step debug run enabled synthetic tests. The first attempt used a per-device batch size of one with two generations. After user approval, the batch size was changed to two and the exact training command was retried.

### Metrics

- The first attempt stopped before training because the generation batch size was not divisible by `num_generations`.
- The retry completed one GRPO step.
- The successful run logged `reward/synthetic_tests/mean = 20`.

### What Went Well

The successful metric established that each sampled task used 20 hidden tests during reward execution.

### What Went Badly

The initial smoke configuration violated TRL's divisibility requirement.

### Learning

For grouped GRPO sampling, the generation batch size must remain compatible with the number of generations even in minimal smoke configurations.

### Next Hypothesis

Run a comparable error-analysis batch with synthetic tests enabled to determine which failure patterns change.

## Cycle 11: Recover the strongest GRPO-only baseline

### Hypothesis

Identifying the exact 40 percent run would provide a reproducible baseline for evaluating broader hidden-test coverage.

### Experiment

Local artifacts and W&B metadata were matched to the strongest GRPO-only evaluation.

### Metrics

- W&B run `wvx9f3bt` reached 0.400 evaluation pass rate.
- The run used commit `5f384a2`, 370 steps, learning rate `1e-6`, four generations, batch size one, gradient accumulation 32, and restored checkpoint 150.

### What Went Well

The baseline was tied to an exact run, checkpoint, commit, and optimization configuration.

### What Went Badly

The final checkpoint was weaker than checkpoint 150, so the result depended on best-checkpoint restoration.

### Learning

The 0.400 baseline is a checkpoint-selected result and should be compared using the same evaluation protocol.

### Next Hypothesis

Use the recovered YAML with synthetic tests enabled and classify every new evaluation failure against the established baseline.

## Agentic Coding

### Workflows and Automation

- The `run-iteratively` workflow stopped on the invalid batch configuration, presented the exact fix, and retried only after approval.
- The `take-meeting-notes` workflow appended the synthetic-test and baseline-recovery cycles to this file.
- Parallel agents analyzed mutation design, task-family documentation, and the strongest GRPO-only configuration.

### Code and Infrastructure Changes

- Commit `6c42bd2` added synthetic MBPP test generation, optional hidden-test loading, reward diagnostics, documentation, tests, and run artifacts.
- Commit `6c42bd2` was pushed to `origin/main` after explicit approval.

### What Worked

The smoke metric provided direct evidence that the optional data path reached reward execution.

### What Failed

The first smoke run failed because the batch size and number of generations were incompatible.

### Learning

Data augmentation should be validated at the reward boundary, and iterative execution should preserve failed configurations as evidence before applying an approved fix.

## Session

- Date: 2026-08-14
- Topic: Constraint-safe synthetic tests and fixed-dense GRPO training.

## Starting Point

The first synthetic artifact executed but contained inputs outside many task contracts. The reward transition also remained enabled in a run intended to use fixed-dense GRPO.

## Cycle 12: Audit unsafe synthetic tests

### Hypothesis

Reference execution alone would reject invalid synthetic inputs.

### Experiment

The artifact was audited for cross-argument and task-domain constraints that reference execution did not enforce.

### Metrics

- The initial artifact contained 6,762 assertions across 374 records.
- Of 691 tests with a container-length parameter, 419 violated the required relationship.
- Eleven tasks contained 60 assertions encoding accidental `None` results.

### What Went Well

The audit identified invalid-input mechanisms before the replacement run.

### What Went Badly

Oracle success admitted malformed matrices, invalid indices, unsorted inputs, invalid dimensions, and accidental reference behavior.

### Learning

Reference consistency does not prove that an input satisfies the task contract.

### Next Hypothesis

Constraint inference and preflight validation can make the artifact safe for training.

## Cycle 13: Restore fixed-dense reward

### Hypothesis

Removing the transition entirely would prevent it from silently returning through configuration.

### Experiment

Commit `c53b195` removed transition fields and logic from training code and both YAML files. It added a fixed-dense regression test.

### Metrics

- Fixed reward reports `reward/pass_weight = 0` and `reward/dense_weight = 1`.
- All 30 repository tests passed.

### What Went Well

The transition was removed at configuration and implementation levels.

### What Went Badly

Run `ik0yc3pv` reached step 217 with the unwanted transition and was invalid for the requested experiment.

### Learning

Critical experiment invariants should be enforced in code and tests.

### Next Hypothesis

The corrected reward can be paired with a validated synthetic artifact.

## Cycle 14: Infer constraints and validate tests

### Hypothesis

Cross-argument, shape, ordering, index, positivity, and oracle constraints could reject malformed variants.

### Experiment

Commit `767b9ca` added constraint inference, constraint-aware generation, a standalone validator, and loader validation. The generator resumed and repaired only failing tasks instead of regenerating every task.

### Metrics

- Validation reported zero errors across 374 records and 1,870 assertions.
- The target changed from `M = 20` to `M = 5` because task 692 has only six novel valid inputs.
- Every record contains exactly five accepted assertions.

### What Went Well

The artifact passed structural, uniqueness, inferred-domain, cross-argument, deterministic-oracle, and coverage checks.

### What Went Badly

Twenty distinct valid variants were infeasible for at least one finite-domain task.

### Learning

The global `M` must respect the smallest novel input space. Resume and targeted repair avoid unnecessary regeneration.

### Next Hypothesis

Five validated hidden tests per task will broaden reward coverage without invalid-domain failures.

## Cycle 15: Start twenty-epoch GRPO

### Hypothesis

Fixed-dense GRPO with validated hidden tests can cleanly test whether wider coverage improves generalization.

### Experiment

Commit `b66b28e` set `configs/default.yaml` to 20 epochs. The run uses 374 training records, all 90 validation examples, five hidden tests per task, and no reward transition.

### Metrics

- W&B run `tiyry19u` is active for 920 configured steps.
- Step 1 confirmed `reward/pass_weight = 0`, `reward/dense_weight = 1`, and `reward/synthetic_tests/mean = 5`.
- Step 1 reward mean was 0.1242.
- No final evaluation result is available.

### What Went Well

The first step confirmed the validated artifact and fixed-dense reward at the reward boundary.

### What Went Badly

The experiment is active, so no final conclusion is justified.

### Learning

The run must remain labeled active until the full validation evaluation completes.

### Next Hypothesis

The final error taxonomy will show whether safe hidden tests improve generalization or only change reward density.

## Agentic Coding

### Skills Created or Updated

No skills were created or modified.

### Workflows and Automation

- The `take-meeting-notes` skill recorded the audit, correction, validation, and active run.
- Targeted repair regenerated only failing records.
- Preflight validation now blocks invalid synthetic data from training.

### Code and Infrastructure Changes

- Commit `c53b195` enforced fixed dense reward.
- Commit `767b9ca` added constraint-aware generation and validation.
- Commit `b66b28e` configured the 20-epoch experiment.

### What Worked

Validation produced 1,870 assertions with zero reported errors. Targeted repair reduced unnecessary generation.

### What Failed

Reference-only validation and a universal target of 20 variants did not respect task contracts or finite domains.

### Learning

Synthetic generation needs explicit domain validation, exact coverage checks, and resumable repair before training.


## Cycle 16: Align evaluation and training rewards

### Commit Name

Align evaluation and training rewards.

### Description

Evaluation now uses the same dense reward scorer as GRPO training. Binary pass rate remains separate, and W&B also receives the literal `evaluation/pass@1` metric. Focused tests cover partial reward without a full pass.

### Why

The previous evaluation average reward was binary and duplicated pass@1. Matching the training reward makes checkpoint comparisons meaningful while preserving the correctness metric.

### Context

The change follows the review of W&B run `sparkling-sunset-30`, where the metric mismatch made the apparent training and evaluation divergence difficult to interpret. The repository instructions now require this context for every commit.


## Cycle 17: Prioritize full-pass reward

### Commit Name

Prioritize full-pass reward and baseline evaluation.

### Description

The reward now uses rounded coefficients of format 0.025, syntax 0.05, interface 0.025, partial-test progress 0.4, and full pass 0.5. The separate execution reward was removed. Default training now recomputes the baseline evaluation and runs for ten epochs.

### Why

Full correctness is the primary target, while the remaining components only provide within-batch diversity. Recomputing the baseline makes every run comparable.

### Context

This is the approved experiment following the W&B analysis of sparkling-sunset-30. It uses the full validation set and will be monitored through iterative error analysis.


## Cycle 18: Select and stop by evaluation pass rate

### Commit Name

Select checkpoints by evaluation pass and stop after patience exhaustion.

### Description

Checkpoint ranking now accepts the W&B-facing `pass` name and maps it to the stored `pass_at_1` metric. The default configuration enables a patience of three checkpoint evaluations without a strictly higher pass. Such a run requests trainer termination while retaining the best checkpoint. Focused tests cover the stopping behavior.

### Why

Held-out pass is the target metric, while cumulative training reward can plateau without generalization. Patience stopping prevents continued training after three non-improving evaluations.

### Context

This change implements the requested checkpoint selection and early stopping policy for the current full-validation training experiment. Generated logs and results remain uncommitted.


## Cycle 19: Add evaluation diagnostics

### Commit Name

Log evaluation diagnostics for reward alignment and exploration.

### Description

Evaluation metrics now include reward-component means, successful-example counts, unique-completion rate, repeated-completion fraction, token entropy, and optional reference-policy KL. These metrics are passed through the existing W&B evaluation logger.

### Why

The additional measurements distinguish reward misalignment, evaluation noise, and exploration collapse instead of inferring causes from pass@1 alone.

### Context

Entropy and diversity are measured on generated evaluation completions. Reference KL is recorded when the evaluated model exposes a reference policy; otherwise it is omitted because evaluating a separate reference model would change the experiment cost and setup.


# Session: 2026-08-15

- Topic: Diagnose reward plateaus, exploration collapse, and improve evaluation observability.

## Starting Point

W&B run `sparkling-sunset-30` showed training reward increasing while evaluation pass@1 and average reward improved only modestly. The active follow-up run was `glowing-serenity-31` with run ID `gj8wan0y`, full validation over 90 examples, and ten configured epochs.

## Cycle 20: Audit the evaluation and training objective

### Hypothesis

The apparent gap could reflect a metric mismatch rather than model behavior.

### Experiment

Evaluation was changed to use the same dense scorer as training, while retaining binary pass@1 as a separate correctness metric. The reward coefficients were rounded to format 0.025, syntax 0.05, interface 0.025, partial-test progress 0.4, and full pass 0.5.

### Metrics

For `glowing-serenity-31`, baseline evaluation was average reward 0.2713889 and pass@1 0.1777778. Later checkpoint values were step 50 average reward 0.3023148 and pass@1 0.2111111, step 75 average reward 0.3119444 and pass@1 0.2222222, and step 100 average reward 0.2997222 and pass@1 0.2111111.

### What Went Well

The evaluation average reward now has the same semantics as the training reward. W&B receives both `evaluation/average_reward` and `evaluation/pass@1`.

### What Went Badly

The validation set contains 90 examples, so changes of four successful examples are noisy. The W&B automatic divergence diagnosis was also unreliable because it selected a profiling timing metric instead of a loss metric.

### Learning

Pass@1 remains the primary model-selection metric. Average reward is useful for diagnosis, but it cannot replace full correctness.

### Next Hypothesis

Separate reward misalignment, evaluation noise, and exploration collapse with direct diagnostics rather than interpreting reward curves alone.

## Cycle 21: Evaluate exploration-collapse and reward-misalignment hypotheses

### Hypothesis

A falling policy entropy may indicate that the model is becoming confident in a narrow set of unsuccessful behaviors, but entropy decline alone does not establish causality.

### Experiment

The run was inspected for entropy, reward variance, group diversity, and pass@1. Standard possible interventions were identified as a small entropy bonus, KL constraint, lower learning rate, more generations per prompt, or higher sampling temperature. A 90% pass@1 reward was considered as a separate objective-alignment experiment.

### Metrics

Entropy fell from approximately 1.50 early in training to approximately 0.55 to 0.66 by trainer step 114. Training average reward was approximately 0.1931 cumulatively and 0.2141 in the rolling window at the observed point. Group diversity remained nonzero, and full-pass fraction varied across batches.

### What Went Well

The entropy decline is a concrete warning signal and motivated targeted observability. The prior binary full-pass reward experiment did not materially improve pass@1, so reward weighting alone is not established as the root cause.

### What Went Badly

There is not enough evidence to distinguish harmful exploration collapse from normal entropy reduction during learning. Evaluation noise and partial-credit reward optimization remain viable explanations.

### Learning

Exploration collapse is a leading hypothesis, not a conclusion. Reward misalignment would be supported if dense reward or partial-test progress rises while full-pass reward and pass@1 remain flat.

### Next Hypothesis

At each evaluation, log reward components, success counts, completion repetition, entropy, and reference-policy KL when available.

## Cycle 22: Add evaluation diagnostics

### Hypothesis

Direct evaluation diagnostics will distinguish partial-credit optimization from reduced exploration and noisy pass-rate changes.

### Experiment

Commit `e43b8a0` added evaluation metrics for reward-component means, successful examples, unique completion rate, repeated completion fraction, token entropy, and optional reference-policy KL. These metrics use the existing W&B evaluation logging path. Commit `15a7da6` separately made pass@1 control checkpoint selection and added patience stopping after three non-improving checkpoint evaluations.

### Metrics

The full test suite passed with 44 tests after the diagnostics change.

### What Went Well

The diagnostics are persisted in evaluation metric artifacts and are automatically flattened into W&B keys such as `evaluation/reward_tests_mean`, `evaluation/entropy`, and `evaluation/unique_completion_rate`.

### What Went Badly

The currently running process was launched before the diagnostics code was committed, so it will not emit the new fields without a restart. Reference KL is omitted when the evaluated model does not expose a reference policy.

### Learning

Reward misalignment should be audited by comparing component trends, passed-versus-failed reward distributions, and within-prompt reward ranking. Evaluation noise should be audited with bootstrap intervals or repeated samples. Exploration collapse should require aligned declines in entropy, completion diversity, and group variation.

### Next Hypothesis

Run a fresh evaluation-enabled training process and first test whether dense reward increases are explained by partial-test progress rather than full passes. Only then choose between a 90% pass-weight reward and an exploration-preserving intervention.

## Cycle 23: Align W&B evaluation charts

### Hypothesis

The intermediate evaluation rows were logged, but charts were using W&B's internal history row index instead of trainer evaluation steps, making the series appear sparse or misleading.

### Experiment

W&B history for `glowing-serenity-31` was queried directly. The run contains 21 evaluation rows: baseline, checkpoint steps 25 through 450 at 25-step intervals, and final step 460 entries. `train.py` was updated to define `evaluation/step` as the custom step metric for all `evaluation/*` metrics.

### Metrics

The raw history contains pass@1 and average reward at steps 0, 25, 50, 75, 100, 125, 150, 175, 200, 225, 250, 275, 300, 325, 350, 375, 400, 425, 450, and 460.

### What Went Well

The W&B MCP confirmed that intermediate values were not missing from the run. The full test suite passed with 44 tests after the chart-axis change.

### What Went Badly

The default W&B history axis is the global row index, which reached 46,482 because training logs were emitted far more frequently than evaluations. This can obscure evaluation points in the UI.

### Learning

Evaluation logging was functioning. The missing-looking values were a visualization-axis problem rather than an evaluation-callback problem. New runs will associate evaluation metrics with `evaluation/step`.

### Next Hypothesis

A fresh run or newly logged evaluation series should display every checkpoint evaluation at its trainer step.


## Cycle 24: Stop generation at the code fence

### Hypothesis

Most sampled completions are being cut off because the model does not emit EOS before the 512-token limit. Stopping at the required closing code fence should reduce unterminated outputs and improve reward stability.

### Experiment

The approved change adds a Transformers stop string for the closing ````` sequence to GRPO training generation and deterministic evaluation generation. The hard completion cap increases from 512 to 768 as a safety margin. The full-validation, ten-epoch configuration remains otherwise unchanged.

### Metrics

The prior run had 460 training batches and 14,720 sampled completions. 9,516 were unterminated according to `completions/clipped_ratio`, for an overall rate of 64.65 percent. Every batch reached the 512-token maximum.

### What Went Well

The stop rule is applied to both training and evaluation paths. The existing test suite passes with 44 tests.

### What Went Badly

The current run was already finished, so the cutoff fix requires a fresh run. The installed TRL generation path requires a tokenizer argument for stop strings, so a small model-generation wrapper supplies it during GRPO generation.

### Learning

A closing-fence stop is a more direct intervention than only increasing the maximum length. The new run must verify that the clipped ratio falls sharply before attributing any pass@1 change to reward or exploration changes.

### Next Hypothesis

If clipping falls below roughly 10 percent and pass@1 improves, truncation was a primary bottleneck. If clipping remains high, the model is failing to follow the output format and may need SFT or a different stopping implementation.


## Cycle 25: Repair prompt-sensitive stopping

### Hypothesis

The first stop-string implementation may stop on the prompt template rather than generated content because Transformers scans the full sequence.

### Experiment

The first post-fix run `skilled-cosmos-32` was launched with the closing-fence stop and 768-token cap. Its baseline evaluation and first 32 training completions were inspected, then the run was interrupted after the first batch. A token-level stopping criterion was implemented that checks only tokens after the prompt width and tracks completion rows independently.

### Metrics

The failed run produced 90 baseline format errors and 32 first-batch training format errors. The first training batch had zero reward, zero mixed groups, and a reported clipped ratio of 0.125, but all completions were empty after the prompt-sensitive stop fired.

### What Went Well

The failure was detected immediately from the first batch rather than consuming the full ten-epoch budget. The smoke test for the replacement criterion confirms that a fence in prompt tokens does not trigger it, while a fence in generated tokens does. The full repository suite passes with 44 tests.

### What Went Badly

The first implementation was not safe: built-in stop strings matched the example fence already present in every prompt and caused empty completions.

### Learning

Stop conditions must operate on generated-token suffixes, not the complete prompt-plus-completion sequence, when the prompt contains the same delimiter.

### Next Hypothesis

The replacement criterion will preserve generated code, reduce the 64.65 percent unterminated rate, and avoid the all-format-error failure.

## Commit: Log raw and sandbox-ready completions

### Change and Reason

Updated training sample logging in `evaluate.py` to record the raw completion returned by the language model and the extracted code passed to the sandbox after fence extraction and truncation. This makes formatting and stopping failures directly auditable.

### Validation and Context

Reran one GRPO training step with 32 sampled completions. The log contains 32 raw-completion entries and 32 sandbox-input entries, with 11 format errors and zero exact placeholder outputs. The first raw completion included a closing fence followed by extra generated text, while the sandbox input stopped at the first fenced block as intended.

## Commit: Remove placeholder example from prompt

### Change and Reason

Replaced the concrete `def example(): pass` code-fence example in `data.py` with textual instructions requiring one fenced Python implementation and explicitly prohibiting placeholder implementations. Dataset normalization now bypasses the Hugging Face datasets cache so prompt-template edits are applied immediately.

### Validation and Context

Ran a one-step GRPO smoke test with 32 training records and four generations per record. The run emitted 32 sampled completions, including 11 format errors and one real-function `pass`, but zero exact `def example(): pass` placeholders. The prior completed run contained the exact placeholder in 234 of 14,720 training samples, or 1.59 percent. The latest corrected run before this prompt change contained 163 of 800, or 20.4 percent, so the behavior emerged in sampled training outputs immediately at the first logged batch rather than only after many epochs.

## Commit: Force the response code prefix during generation

### Change and Reason

Added a token-level logits processor that forces each completion to begin with `Code:` and an opening Python fence. The existing generated-only stopping criterion remains responsible for stopping at the closing fence. This enforces the response protocol without placing a fake placeholder implementation in the prompt.

### Validation and Context

The first implementation incorrectly treated the forced opening fence as the closing fence and produced 90 format errors. The stopping offset was corrected to begin after the forced prefix. The final 90-example baseline achieved pass@1 0.20, average reward 0.2956, 18 passed, 13 partial, 48 failed, and 11 format errors. Focused evaluation and sandbox tests passed 18/18.

## Commit: Add task-specific function prefixes and arity retry

### Change and Reason

Prompts now state the required function name. Evaluation generation forces `Code:`, the Python fence, and the task-specific function-name prefix while leaving argument names to the model. When the generated function has the correct name but the wrong positional-argument count, evaluation appends the previous completion and the exact correction message, then retries once using `NUM_RETRIES = 1`. The retry structure preserves accumulated context for future additional retries.

### Validation and Context

The 90-example baseline achieved pass@1 0.2889, average reward 0.3689, 26 passed, 11 partial, 43 failed, and 10 format errors. Focused evaluation and sandbox tests passed 18/18. This is an improvement over the previous task-independent forced-prefix baseline of 18 passed and 11 format errors.

## Commit: Preserve raw evaluation completions

### Change and Reason

Evaluation artifacts and human-readable logs now store the raw model completion separately from the extracted sandbox input. Previously malformed evaluation completions appeared as empty strings because only successful fence extraction was persisted, preventing direct diagnosis.

### Validation and Context

The latest baseline's eight parseable interface failures all had the correct task-specific function name but the wrong positional arity. The one retry was invoked for this class of error, but the retried generation still produced an incorrect count. Focused evaluation and sandbox tests passed 18/18.

## Commit: Configure generic argument hints and retry count

### Change and Reason

Made the retry count configurable through `max_retries` and removed the hardcoded retry constant. Added `include_generic_arguments` to control whether prompts include headers such as `def target(arg1, arg2):`. The default experiment now uses zero retries and generic argument hints.

### Validation and Context

The 90-example baseline with zero retries and generic argument hints achieved pass@1 0.2111, average reward 0.2849, 19 passed, 8 partial, 42 failed, 11 format errors, and 10 syntax errors. This was below the previous one-retry, no-generic-header baseline of 26 passed, so generic hints did not improve this deterministic baseline. Focused evaluation and sandbox tests passed 18/18.

## Commit: Prefill complete generic function signatures

### Change and Reason

Changed generic arguments from a prose hint into a prewritten signature. The prompt and generation prefix now contain the complete header, such as `def target(arg1, arg2):`, and the model is instructed and constrained to generate only the body. This prevents the model from copying literal test values into parameter lists.

### Validation and Context

The 90-example baseline produced zero syntax errors, confirming that numeric test values were no longer emitted as parameters. It achieved pass@1 0.20, average reward 0.2763, 18 passed, 11 partial, 39 failed, and 22 format errors. The increased format-error rate shows that prefilled headers currently interact poorly with the closing-fence protocol and require a follow-up formatting fix.

## Experiment: Discourage lookup-table solutions explicitly

### Results

The baseline achieved pass@1 0.2333, average reward 0.3231, 21 passed, 13 partial, 46 failed, 9 format errors, and 1 syntax error.

### Failure Audit

The nine format errors again had a correct opening fence but no closing fence because long repetitive generations hit the 768-token cap. The single syntax error used a malformed list comprehension inside `tuple[...]` instead of a list or tuple constructor. Ten parseable interface failures all had the correct function name but wrong arity. The anti-lookup instruction reduced format errors slightly but did not prevent verbose repetitive solutions or reliably enforce argument counts.

## Experiment: Reaudit option-4 baseline failures

### Results

Reran the 90-example baseline with task-specific function-name forcing, model-generated argument names, and one configurable arity retry. The result reproduced pass@1 0.2889, average reward 0.3689, 26 passed, 11 partial, 43 failed, and 10 format errors.

### Failure Audit

All ten format errors had the correct opening fence but no closing fence because generation hit the 768-token cap. There were zero syntax errors. The eight parseable interface failures all had the correct function name but the wrong positional arity, so the remaining interface problem is argument-count generation rather than name generation. Raw completions and extracted sandbox inputs are now preserved for future audits.

## Experiment: Eliminate syntax and interface errors before RL quality optimization

### Objective

Iterate on the prompt and generation setup until syntax errors and invalid interfaces are eliminated or near zero. These failures are likely caused by suboptimal prompting, task alignment, and response-format behavior. A sufficiently capable small base model should solve the basic MBPP tasks once the protocol is reliable. Reinforcement learning should then focus on improving coding quality rather than teaching the model to emit parseable code.

### Baseline Interface Audit

The forced-prefix baseline had 23 completions without a valid interface. Eleven were format errors and twelve were parseable but exposed the wrong function or arity. Examples included implementations of `min_coins` instead of the requested task, `topbottom_surfacearea` and `surface_area_cylinder` for unrelated surface-area tasks, a `bases_num_coresspondingnum` placeholder, `union_elements(*tuples)` with the wrong calling convention, and `is_empty(my_dict)` for another task. This pattern indicates task or interface alignment failures, not primarily Python syntax difficulty.

### Reward-Hacking Interpretation

### Reward-hacking motivation

## Commit: Restart training with test-pass fraction reward

Changed score_completion and reward_function so the only nonzero reward is passed_tests divided by total_tests. Disabled baseline evaluation in configs/default.yaml and updated focused tests for the new reward semantics.

The motivation for removing format, syntax, interface, and binary-pass bonuses is concern about the earlier reward-hacking pattern. The model may learn useless solutions that are merely syntactically valid when syntax and format rewards provide reliable credit while test progress stagnates or declines. The observed increase in syntax reward alongside flat or falling other reward components supports testing a reward based only on the fraction of test cases passed.

Restored max_completion_length from 3072 to 768 at the user’s direction. Restored evaluation_batch_size to 8 at the user’s direction while keeping per_device_train_batch_size at 1.

Doubled the default completion limit from 1536 to 3072 tokens before launching the requested full 10-epoch training run. The incomplete-fence fallback remains enabled so generated bodies can still be scored when the closing fence is absent.

Doubled the default completion limit from 768 to 1536 tokens and changed code extraction to score the body through end-of-output when an opening Python fence has no closing fence. Added a regression test for the fallback. Focused sandbox and evaluation tests passed 19/19.

The training per-device batch size was already 1, so evaluation_batch_size was reduced from 8 to 1 to reduce evaluation memory pressure without changing the effective training batch.

Recorded the evaluation-only 90-example baseline rerun, its complete interface and protocol error inventory, comparison with the previous baseline, root-cause analysis, and proposed confirmation tests. This preserves the evidence that parseable interface failures fell from 12 to 5 while format and syntax failures increased.

## Experiment: Rerun current full baseline and audit interface errors

Reran configs/default.yaml through the existing evaluation path over all 90 validation examples with Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B, seed 42, task-specific name forcing, model-selected arguments, max_retries 1, and max_completion_length 768. The result was pass@1 0.2556, average reward 0.3371, 23 passed, 13 partial, 38 failed, 12 format errors, and 4 syntax errors. Format-error tasks were 515, 518, 520, 524, 529, 531, 539, 541, 550, 571, 584, and 586. Wrong-arity tasks were 519, 521, 547, 578, and 580. Syntax-error tasks were 513, 533, 568, and 583. Parseable interface failures fell from 12 to 5, but format errors rose from 10 to 12 and syntax errors rose from zero to four. The next fix should enforce compact parseable signatures and bodies before further RL quality changes.

The `def example(): pass` behavior was not caused by the prompt alone or by reward alone. The prompt supplied a concrete placeholder that the model could copy, creating an exploitable low-effort completion. The dense reward then amplified that behavior because a copied placeholder earned format and syntax credit while avoiding difficult tests. Evidence for this interaction is that the placeholder existed at low frequency before the stopping changes, rose sharply in training samples, and disappeared from the one-batch smoke test after removing the example. The correct interpretation is prompt-induced exploitability amplified by reward optimization, rather than an intrinsic model preference independent of the prompt.

## Proposed clean self-training iteration

I would stop trying miscellaneous hyperparameter tweaks for the moment and run one clean iteration:

Generate 16 on the entire MBPP training set, execute, retain one or several passing solutions, SFT the 0.8B model on those solutions, and reevaluate pass@1 and pass@16.

If pass@1 jumps while pass@16 stays roughly around 80%, the best-of-16 performance has been compressed into the policy. That is the desired direction.

Then repeat the generation and training loop. Eventually the model will exhaust the problems it can self-solve. At that point, use a stronger model to provide solutions for the remaining 0/16 bucket.

The 20% pass@1 and 80% pass@16 gap is much more encouraging than a 20% and 25% gap. The latter would suggest the 0.8B model is near its capability ceiling. The former says there is substantial recoverable performance already present in the distribution.

## 2026-09-05: Rank validation generations by baseline likelihood

Scored the 16 saved task 511 validation completions with the same Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B baseline model and ranked them by total generated-token log-likelihood. Logged each rank with generation number, total and mean log-likelihood, token count, status, and test-pass percentage. The fully correct generation 13 ranked 13th, while generation 14 ranked first with 33.3% test coverage, showing that raw likelihood is not a reliable correctness reranker in this sample.

## 2026-09-05: Normalize generation likelihood by token count

Reran likelihood scoring on the same 16 task 511 validation generations using mean token log-likelihood. The fully correct generation 13 ranked first at -0.2734, followed by generation 5 at -0.2891. The normalized ranking was appended to logs/logs.txt and aligns better with test correctness than total log-likelihood.

## 2026-09-05: Add reusable likelihood-ranking evaluator

Promoted the mean-token-log-likelihood scorer from a temporary experiment script into `rank_generations.py`. The script loads saved generation details, rescored all candidates with the same model, preserves original generation order, computes original and reranked pass@1, pass@2, pass@4, pass@8, and pass@16, writes complete per-example rankings to JSON, prints aggregate statistics, and appends the summary to `logs/logs.txt`. Re-running the 20-example validation artifact reproduced original-order rates of 5%, 10%, 15%, 15%, and 30%, and reranked rates of 10%, 15%, 15%, 25%, and 30%. Focused tests passed 21/21.

## 2026-09-05: Record reusable scorer verification log update

## 2026-09-05: Reduce syntax failures with body-start and protocol guards

Changed evaluation decoding to prefill the indented function body, block immediate closing fences and protocol or reasoning markers until a meaningful body line appears, and select the first syntactically valid nonempty Python fence when multiple fences are present. Reran validation task 513 with 16 generations, generation microbatch size 2, and a 768-token completion budget. Syntax errors fell from 10/16 before the change to 3/16 after the change. The remaining three errors were truncated long implementations, while four candidates became partial and nine remained full failures. Pass@16 remained 0/16, so formatting recovery improved but did not solve task correctness.

## Correction: 52.2% execution-first result is not hidden-test accuracy

The 52.2% selected-candidate result from the 90-example run was computed by selecting candidates using the prompt-visible tests and scoring them on those same tests. It is therefore test-aware visible-test selection performance, not hidden-test generalization accuracy. The current evaluator uses `record["test_code"]`, built from the prompt's `test_list`; hidden synthetic tests are included only when `synthetic_tests_enabled: true`, and MBPP `challenge_test_list` is not currently wired into evaluation. Do not use 52.2% as a hidden-test result.

## 2026-09-06 Official Qwen prompt comparison

Compared the evaluator with Qwen3-Coder's official EvalPlus prompt and generation setup. The official code wraps a function-context prompt in the ChatML system message, uses a concrete Python function stub, prefills ```python, samples with top_p 0.95, and allows 2048 new tokens. Our first attempted official mode still supplied the repository's prose prompt without a function stub and unnecessarily computed likelihoods during standard pass@K evaluation. The corrected evaluator now reconstructs a function stub from the public task interface and description, uses the test-free prompt, skips unused likelihood scoring, and supports top_p.

The corrected 10-example Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B-Instruct diagnostic with 20 samples, temperature 0.7, top_p 0.95, 1024 prompt tokens, and 2048 completion tokens reached pass@1 30%, pass@2 30%, pass@4 50%, pass@8 60%, pass@10 60%, pass@16 60%, and pass@20 70%. Its 200 candidates had 61 full passes, 11 partial failures, and 128 full behavioral failures, with all interfaces valid. A full 90-example rerun was stopped at 15 examples after pass@1 remained 20% and pass@20 was 53.33%. Exclusion filtering was not run because the requested baseline pass@1 threshold above 50% was not reached.

The remaining major comparability difference is the benchmark. Qwen's official script evaluates EvalPlus MBPP+ task prompts, while this repository evaluates a 90-row Hugging Face MBPP validation subset with its own sandbox and visible hidden-test execution. The official Qwen repository does not establish a greater-than-50% pass@1 result for this 0.5B model under this repository's protocol.

## 2026-09-06 Exa API-key authentication

Stopped the GRPO run at the user's request. Its step-10 checkpoint had original pass@1 32.22% and synthetic-selection pass@1 36.67%, below the cached baseline selection pass@1 40%. The next launch was corrected to use a TRL-valid generation batch size of 16, but training was later stopped before the step-20 checkpoint completed.

Configured Exa MCP to use the hosted endpoint with an environment-backed x-api-key header. Added EXA_API_KEY prompting and persistence to start.sh alongside the existing service-key prompts. The key remains only in the ignored local .env and is excluded from notes, logs, and Git.

## 2026-09-06 Qwen2.5-Coder zero-shot rerun

Investigated the Qwen3-Coder issue about underperforming OpenCompass results, Qwen's Qwen2.5-Coder technical report, the Qwen model card, the Qwen EvalPlus evaluation repository, and the EvalPlus paper. The issue concerns Qwen2.5-Coder-3B and does not directly validate a 0.5B diagnosis. Qwen's report lists Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B-Instruct at 61.6% HumanEval, 57.3% HumanEval+, 52.4% MBPP, and 43.7% MBPP+.

Reran the repository's 90-example MBPP validation evaluation with exactly one sampled completion per task, temperature 0.2, top_p 0.95, no visible tests, a 512-token completion cap, and generation in batches of eight. The result was 27/90 passed, or 30.00% pass@1, with 9 partial and 54 failed examples. The saved artifact is outputs/eval-qwen25coder-zero-shot-90-1-temp02/baseline-metrics.json.

Interpretation: adopting EvalPlus is plausible as a source of benchmark comparability problems, but it would not by itself explain a low score because EvalPlus adds stricter tests and usually lowers scores. The larger mismatches are that this repository uses the 90-row validation split rather than Qwen's reported benchmark split, uses a custom scorer and sandbox, and uses a repository-specific prompt and code-prefix protocol. The rerun confirms that removing visible tests and using one sample at 0.2 does not recover the reported low-50s result.

## 2026-09-07: 7B vLLM memory adjustment

The Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct EvalPlus launch with batch size 1 failed during vLLM engine initialization because the 16 GiB GPU could not satisfy the evaluator's 0.98 GPU memory reservation. Reduced only vLLM's gpu_memory_utilization setting from 0.98 to 0.90 so the 7B run can initialize while keeping the model, prompt, sampling, sequence length, and scoring protocol unchanged.


## 2026-09-07: 7B context-length compatibility adjustment

The clean 7B retry still failed during vLLM profiling because the 14.2488 GiB bfloat16 weights left too little memory for the 1536-token dummy profile on the 16 GiB GPU. Added a 7B-only max_model_len of 1024 while retaining the official model, prompt, stop strings, sampling temperature, top-p, and ten samples per task. This is a hardware compatibility adjustment and is recorded as a protocol limitation for the 7B comparison.


## 2026-09-07: 7B CPU offload compatibility adjustment

The 7B-only 1024-token retry still failed during vLLM profiling because the standard bfloat16 weights consume 14.2488 GiB and leave insufficient workspace on the 16 GiB GPU. Added 2 GiB of vLLM CPU weight offload for the 7B variant. This preserves the standard checkpoint and sampling behavior, but the 7B result will carry a hardware-offload caveat.


## 2026-09-07: CodeBERT verifier training module

Added train_verifier.py as a dedicated task-conditioned correctness-verifier pipeline. It loads MBPP, splits tasks before candidate generation, labels reference implementations as correct, generates configurable Qwen candidates, labels candidates by full sandbox test success, trains microsoft/codebert-base with BCEWithLogitsLoss, logs train and validation loss, accuracy, positive rate, and ROC AUC to W&B each epoch, and saves JSONL data plus the verifier checkpoint and metrics. The focused verifier smoke check passed. Existing tests passed except test_prepare_datasets_loads_official_train_and_validation_splits, which fails because the existing test double accepts the old three-argument load_mbpp signature while data.py currently passes the include_generic_arguments argument.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier quarter-epoch evaluation

Extended train_verifier.py with evaluation of the saved sandbox-labeled validation candidates. The verifier now uses a 0.5 probability threshold, logs train and eval loss, accuracy, positive rate, and AUC at four evenly spaced checkpoints per epoch, saves per-example probabilities and yes or no predictions, and supports --eval-only to reload the checkpoint and validation JSONL without regenerating labels.


## 2026-09-07: Parallel verifier candidate labeling

Kept the original verifier dataset size of one reference plus four generated candidates per MBPP task. Stopped the initial serial run after it spent over twenty minutes in sandbox labeling. Updated train_verifier.py to queue all generated candidates and label them with a bounded ThreadPoolExecutor, defaulting to 16 workers, while emitting task-generation and labeling progress. The generator remains on GPU, while sandbox subprocess execution is intentionally CPU-bound.


## 2026-09-07: Durable verifier labels and monitored training

Changed verifier preparation to save train.jsonl immediately after its labels are complete and validation.jsonl immediately after its labels are complete. Added cache reuse and a prepare-data mode so reruns train from committed labels without regenerating candidates. Added positive-class precision, recall, and F1 to every train and eval checkpoint, retained eval loss and accuracy logging, changed the default to ten epochs, and added early stopping after three consecutive non-improving eval-F1 checkpoints. The planned run will use four generated candidates plus one reference per task and will be retried at most five times if the monitored evaluation fails to improve.


## 2026-09-07: Durable verifier label dataset

Prepared and sandbox-labeled the full task-disjoint MBPP verifier dataset using four generated candidates plus one reference per task. The train split contains 299 tasks and 1,495 labeled examples. The validation split contains 75 tasks and 375 labeled examples. All 1,496 generated candidates were labeled with 16 parallel workers, and each split was saved immediately after labeling.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier cycle 1 diagnosis and cycle 2 fix

The first verifier run stopped after three non-improving eval-F1 checkpoints. The saved best checkpoint had validation accuracy 69.3%, precision 77.3%, recall 56.8%, F1 65.5%, and AUC 77.3%. Label balance was nearly even at 192 positive and 183 negative validation examples, so imbalance was not the cause. Later checkpoints improved loss and AUC but became overly conservative at the fixed 0.5 threshold, ending with precision 100% and recall 39.6%. Added configurable positive-class BCE weighting with a default of 1.5 to penalize false negatives and improve fixed-threshold recall. The next run reuses the committed labels.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier cycle 2 diagnosis and cycle 3 fix

Cycle 2 used positive-class weight 1.5 and improved the best fixed-threshold validation result to accuracy 70.1%, precision 71.5%, recall 69.3%, F1 70.4%, and AUC 77.7%. It nevertheless stopped after later F1 declines while AUC rose to 80.3%, confirming continued probability sharpening and threshold-calibration drift. Added initialization of the classifier output bias to the training positive prior and lowered the learning rate from 2e-5 to 5e-6. The committed labels and positive-class weight remain unchanged.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier cycle 3 diagnosis and cycle 4 fix

Cycle 3 used prior-positive-bias initialization and learning rate 5e-6. Its best eval checkpoint reached accuracy 63.2%, precision 59.7%, recall 86.5%, F1 70.6%, and AUC 77.3%, but subsequent checkpoints overfit the fixed 0.5 threshold and triggered the three-evaluation stop. Cycle 4 freezes the CodeBERT encoder by default and trains only the classification head, testing whether full-encoder updates are unstable on the small 1,870-example verifier dataset.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier cycle 4 diagnosis and cycle 5 configuration

Cycle 4 froze CodeBERT and trained only the classification head. AUC rose from 44.6% to 69.8%, but every checkpoint predicted all validation examples positive at threshold 0.5, leaving accuracy at 51.2% and F1 at 67.7%. This is underfitting rather than a useful calibration fix. The final allowed cycle restores full encoder fine-tuning, retains prior-bias initialization and learning rate 5e-6, and uses positive-class weight 1.25 instead of 1.5.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier five-cycle improvement loop completed

Completed the requested maximum of five verifier improvement cycles using the committed labels and ten-epoch maximum with quarter-epoch evaluation and three-checkpoint eval-F1 patience. Cycle 1 baseline full fine-tuning reached best eval accuracy 69.3%, precision 77.3%, recall 56.8%, F1 65.5%, and AUC 77.3%. Cycle 2 with positive BCE weight 1.5 reached best eval accuracy 70.1%, precision 71.5%, recall 69.3%, F1 70.4%, and AUC 77.7%. Cycle 3 with prior-positive bias initialization and learning rate 5e-6 reached best eval accuracy 63.2%, precision 59.7%, recall 86.5%, F1 70.6%, and AUC 77.3%. Cycle 4 froze the encoder and underfit, remaining at 51.2% accuracy and 67.7% F1 while AUC rose from 44.6% to 69.8%. Cycle 5 restored full fine-tuning with positive weight 1.25 and reached 69.1% accuracy, 83.9% precision, 49.0% recall, 61.8% F1, and 77.8% AUC before stopping. The main diagnosis is probability calibration drift at the required 0.5 threshold: loss and AUC often improve while recall and F1 worsen. W&B runs were created for all cycles, including cycle 5 at https://wandb.ai/kunal-personal/mbpp-verifier/runs/h2dqiusd. No sixth cycle was started because the configured maximum was reached.


## 2026-09-07: Verifier cycle metric clarification

The five-cycle summary reports metrics at each cycle's best eval-F1 checkpoint unless otherwise stated. Cycle 2's maximum thresholded eval accuracy was 71.2% at its third checkpoint, while its best-F1 checkpoint was 70.1% accuracy. Cycle 3's maximum eval accuracy was 69.9%, while its best-F1 checkpoint was 63.2% accuracy. Cycle 5's maximum eval accuracy was 69.9%.

## 2026-09-07: Fix synthetic candidate label extraction

After rerunning with reference non-function helpers and candidate interface validation, all 30 candidates failed, producing Pass@1 through Pass@10 of 0.0. Saved tasks, candidates, and labels under outputs/synthetic-exemplar-task601-3. The result indicates these generated tasks are currently too difficult or contract-misaligned for Qwen and should not be added to verifier training without review.


## 2026-09-08: Simplify exemplar-conditioned task prompt

The first three-task preview produced zero Qwen 0.5B passes. Failure analysis found that the GPT prompt allowed helper classes, missing imports, and interval-chain complexity inherited from exemplar 601. The next iteration constrains each task to one plain function with no imports, classes, annotations, helper functions, recursion, dynamic programming, interval scheduling, or custom objects, and requests a substantially simpler list, string, dictionary, or scalar transformation.


## 2026-09-08: Reject exemplar drift in synthetic tasks

Iteration 2 used the simpler prompt and improved the three-task preview from 0% to 33.3% Pass@10, but all tasks reused max_chain_length and one retained interval-chain semantics. Added AST and semantic validation to reject exemplar-name reuse, interval/pair/chain/subsequence/dynamic-programming/greedy drift, helper classes, imports, annotations, and non-assert test setup before acceptance.


## 2026-09-08: Reach target synthetic-task difficulty

Iteration 3 used hard drift and contract validation and produced three simple counting tasks. Qwen 0.5B passed 19 of 30 candidates, with task-level counts 9, 0, and 10, giving Pass@1 66.7% and Pass@10 66.7%. Iteration 4 used the same pipeline and produced three more accepted tasks. Qwen passed 0, 1, and 7 candidates, giving Pass@1 33.3% and Pass@10 66.7%. Across the six clean accepted tasks from iterations 3 and 4, aggregate Pass@1 is 50.0% and aggregate Pass@10 is 66.7%, meeting the requested target. Candidate files and labels are saved under outputs/synthetic-exemplar-task601-clean-3 and outputs/synthetic-exemplar-task601-clean-4.


## 2026-09-08: Add five-batch synthetic generation and labeling pipeline

Added synthetic_data/batch_generate_label.py. It processes all 1,263 clean verifier exemplars in five deterministic batches, generates three validated GPT-5.4 Mini tasks per exemplar, immediately samples ten Qwen 0.5B candidates per newly generated task with the copied EvalPlus ChatML/vLLM wrapper, labels candidates in the sandbox, saves each batch, and reports cumulative Pass@K and estimated GPT cost every five minutes and after each batch.


## 2026-09-08: Bound full synthetic pipeline spend

Added a $25 maximum GPT generation budget and a maximum of 12 requests per exemplar to batch_generate_label.py. This prevents validation retries from becoming unbounded while preserving five-batch streaming generation, Qwen sampling, labeling, and Pass@K reporting.


## 2026-09-08: Remove legacy generated synthetic examples

Removed the old production synthetic corpus, synthetic verifier candidates, prior synthetic previews, batched synthetic outputs, and the contaminated combined verifier training file. Preserved all source code, official EvalPlus artifacts, and reconstructed outputs/clean-verifier/original-exemplars.jsonl containing only the 263 original MBPP training tasks disjoint from EvalPlus for the replacement run.


## 2026-09-08: Fix original exemplar schema handling

The first launch of the replacement pipeline processed zero exemplars because the reconstructed original-only exemplar artifact does not include a source field. Stopped before any GPT generation and updated batch_generate_label.py to treat missing source as reference while still excluding explicit generated rows.

## September 8, 2026: Autonomous verifier fine-tuning loop

Added an outer-loop driver that fine-tunes the full CodeBERT encoder, monitors evaluation AUC, stops after two stale evaluations, records a diagnosis, changes one optimization setting, and reruns automatically for at most ten outer cycles.

## September 8, 2026: Finalize W&B runs between outer cycles

Updated the autonomous verifier loop to explicitly mark each W&B run finished or crashed after its subprocess exits, including plateau-triggered termination, before the next cycle begins.

## September 8, 2026: Resume outer loop after interruption

Updated the autonomous loop to resume after the last recorded cycle, preventing completed fine-tuning cycles from being rerun when the driver is restarted to apply W&B lifecycle fixes.

## September 8, 2026: Handle forced W&B termination state

Corrected the outer-loop lifecycle update to use W&B’s valid failed state for plateau-triggered subprocess termination.

## September 8, 2026: Fine-tune only upper CodeBERT layers

Added a top-layer fine-tuning mode that freezes embeddings and lower CodeBERT layers while training the top two Transformer layers and classifier. The autonomous loop now uses this mode for its next cycle after full-encoder overfitting.

## September 8, 2026: Extend verifier outer loop

Raised the autonomous diagnosis and rerun cap from ten to twenty outer cycles at the user’s request.

## 2026-09-10: Record ground-truth isolation rule

Added an experiment protocol section to README.md stating that candidate selection must use only answer-independent evidence. Ground truth can be read only afterward for a separate benchmark measurement, never to select or filter candidates from the same evaluation task.

## 2026-09-10: Require reproducible experiment result records

Updated AGENTS.md and synchronized the same rule to the other two local repositories. Metric-bearing experiments must save the experiment purpose, complete setup, artifact identifiers, selection procedure, metrics, environment details, code and commit identifiers, and reproduction limitations in the repository results file. Ground truth remains prohibited during candidate selection and may be used only for a separate post-selection benchmark measurement.
